Grant">Grant</a> agreed to submit the matter to a bipartisan <a href="/wiki/Electoral_Commission_(United_States)" title="Electoral Commission (United States)">Electoral Commission</a>, which would be authorized to determine the fate of the disputed electoral votes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2001157_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2001157-124">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> The Commission was to be made up of five <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="United States House of Representatives">representatives</a>, five <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">senators</a>, and five <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States" title="Supreme Court of the United States">Supreme Court justices</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2001158_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2001158-125">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> To ensure partisan balance, there would be seven Democrats and seven Republicans, with Justice <a href="/wiki/David_Davis_(Supreme_Court_justice)" title="David Davis (Supreme Court justice)">David Davis</a>, an independent respected by both parties, as the 15th member.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2001158_125-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2001158-125">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> The balance was upset when Democrats in the <a href="/wiki/Illinois_State_Legislature" class="mw-redirect" title="Illinois State Legislature">Illinois legislature</a> elected Davis to the Senate, hoping to sway his vote.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995286_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995286-126">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> Davis disappointed Democrats by refusing to serve on the Commission because of his election to the Senate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995286_126-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995286-126">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> As all the remaining Justices were Republicans, Justice <a href="/wiki/Joseph_P._Bradley" title="Joseph P. Bradley">Joseph P. Bradley</a>, believed to be the most independent-minded of them, was selected to take Davis's place on the Commission.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2001159–161_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2001159–161-127">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> The Commission met in February and the eight Republicans voted to award all 20 electoral votes to Hayes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2001166–171_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2001166–171-128">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> Democrats, outraged by the result, attempted a <a href="/wiki/Filibuster" title="Filibuster">filibuster</a> to prevent Congress from accepting the Commission's findings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2001171–183_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2001171–183-129">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Eventually, the filibusterers gave up, allowing the House and Senate to reassemble to complete the count of the electoral votes, and at 4:10 am on March 2, Senator <a href="/wiki/Thomas_W._Ferry" title="Thomas W. Ferry">Thomas W. Ferry</a> announced that Hayes and Wheeler had been elected to the presidency and vice presidency by an electoral margin of 185–184.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As inauguration day neared, Republican and Democratic Congressional leaders met at <a href="/wiki/Wormley%27s_Hotel" title="Wormley&#39;s Hotel">Wormley's Hotel</a> in Washington to negotiate a <a href="/wiki/Compromise_of_1877" title="Compromise of 1877">compromise</a>. Republicans promised concessions in exchange for Democratic acquiescence to the Committee's decision. The main concession Hayes promised was the withdrawal of federal troops from the South and an acceptance of the election of Democratic governments in the remaining "unredeemed" southern states.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2001182–184Foner2002580–581_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2001182–184Foner2002580–581-131">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> The Democrats agreed, and on March 2, the filibuster was ended. Hayes was elected, but Reconstruction was finished, and freedmen were left at the mercy of white Democrats who did not intend to preserve their rights.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2001185–189Foner2002581–587_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2001185–189Foner2002581–587-132">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> On April 3, Hayes ordered Secretary of War <a href="/wiki/George_W._McCrary" title="George W. McCrary">George W. McCrary</a> to withdraw federal troops stationed at the <a href="/wiki/South_Carolina_State_House" title="South Carolina State House">South Carolina State House</a> to their barracks. On April 20, he ordered McCrary to send the federal troops stationed at <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans" title="New Orleans">New Orleans</a>'s St. Louis Hotel to <a href="/wiki/Jackson_Barracks" title="Jackson Barracks">Jackson Barracks</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDodds2013113Clendenen1969246_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDodds2013113Clendenen1969246-133">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h2><span id="Presidency_.281877.E2.80.931881.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidency_(1877–1881)">Presidency (1877–1881)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Presidency (1877–1881)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_Rutherford_B._Hayes" title="Presidency of Rutherford B. Hayes">Presidency of Rutherford B. Hayes</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Inauguration">Inauguration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Inauguration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hayes-inaugurations.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A large crowd of people outside the United States Capitol building" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Hayes-inaugurations.jpg/220px-Hayes-inaugurations.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="281" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Hayes-inaugurations.jpg/330px-Hayes-inaugurations.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Hayes-inaugurations.jpg/440px-Hayes-inaugurations.jpg 2x" data-file-width="470" data-file-height="601" /></a><figcaption>Chief Justice <a href="/wiki/Morrison_R._Waite" class="mw-redirect" title="Morrison R. Waite">Morrison R. Waite</a> administering the oath of office to Hayes</figcaption></figure>
<p>Because March 4, 1877, was a Sunday, Hayes took the oath of office privately on Saturday, March 3, in the <a href="/wiki/Red_Room_(White_House)" title="Red Room (White House)">Red Room</a> of the <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a>, the first president to do so in the Executive Mansion. He took the oath publicly on March 5 on the East Portico of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Capitol" title="United States Capitol">United States Capitol</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995295–297_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995295–297-134">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> In his inaugural address, Hayes attempted to soothe the passions of the past few months, saying that "he serves his party best who serves his country best".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETrefousse200285–86_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETrefousse200285–86-135">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> He pledged to support "wise, honest, and peaceful local self-government" in the South, as well as reform of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_civil_service" class="mw-redirect" title="United States civil service">civil service</a> and a full return to the <a href="/wiki/Gold_standard" title="Gold standard">gold standard</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995298–299_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995298–299-136">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Despite his message of conciliation, many Democrats never considered Hayes's election legitimate and referred to him as "Rutherfraud" or "His Fraudulency" for the next four years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005402–403_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005402–403-137">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="The_South_and_the_end_of_Reconstruction">The South and the end of Reconstruction</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: The South and the end of Reconstruction">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Hayes had firmly supported Republican <a href="/wiki/Reconstruction_era_of_the_United_States" class="mw-redirect" title="Reconstruction era of the United States">Reconstruction</a> policies throughout his career, but the first major act of his presidency was an end to Reconstruction and the return of the South to "home rule".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETrefousse200290–93_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETrefousse200290–93-138">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> Even without the conditions of the Wormley's Hotel agreement, Hayes would have been hard-pressed to continue his predecessors' policies. The House of Representatives in the <a href="/wiki/45th_United_States_Congress" title="45th United States Congress">45th Congress</a> was controlled by a majority of Democrats, and they refused to appropriate enough funds for the army to continue to garrison the South.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995304–307Foner2002580–583Davison1972142_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995304–307Foner2002580–583Davison1972142-139">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> Even among Republicans, devotion to continued military Reconstruction was fading in the face of persistent Southern insurgency and violence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972138Trefousse200292_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972138Trefousse200292-140">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> Only two states, South Carolina and Louisiana, were still under Reconstruction's sway when Hayes assumed the presidency on March 5. On April 3, Hayes ordered Secretary of War <a href="/wiki/George_W._McCrary" title="George W. McCrary">George W. McCrary</a> to withdraw federal troops stationed at the <a href="/wiki/South_Carolina_State_House" title="South Carolina State House">South Carolina State House</a> to their barracks. On April 20, he ordered McCrary to send the federal troops stationed at <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans" title="New Orleans">New Orleans</a>'s St. Louis Hotel to <a href="/wiki/Jackson_Barracks" title="Jackson Barracks">Jackson Barracks</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDodds2013113Clendenen1969246_133-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDodds2013113Clendenen1969246-133">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<p>At the time of the 1876 election only three states, Florida, South Carolina, and Louisiana, still had Republican governments. In Florida the Democrats won the governor's election and controlled the state house, leaving South Carolina and Louisiana as the only states where Republican regimes were still supported by federal troops.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETrefousse200290–91_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETrefousse200290–91-141">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> Without troops to enforce the voting rights laws, these soon fell to Democratic control.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClendenen1969244_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClendenen1969244-142">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hayes's later attempts to protect the rights of southern blacks were ineffective, as were his attempts to rebuild Republican strength in the South.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995317–318Davison1972141–143_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995317–318Davison1972141–143-143">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> But he did defeat Congress's efforts to curtail federal power to monitor federal elections.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972162–163Hoogenboom1995392–402Richardson2001161_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972162–163Hoogenboom1995392–402Richardson2001161-144">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> Democrats in Congress passed an army <a href="/wiki/Appropriations_bill_(United_States)" title="Appropriations bill (United States)">appropriation bill</a> in 1879 with a <a href="/wiki/Rider_(legislation)" title="Rider (legislation)">rider</a> that would have repealed the <a href="/wiki/Enforcement_Acts" title="Enforcement Acts">Enforcement Acts</a>, which had been used to suppress the <a href="/wiki/Ku_Klux_Klan" title="Ku Klux Klan">Ku Klux Klan</a>. Chapters had flourished across the South and it had been one of the insurgent groups that attacked and suppressed freedmen.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972162–163Hoogenboom1995392–402Richardson2001161_144-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972162–163Hoogenboom1995392–402Richardson2001161-144">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> Those Acts, passed during Reconstruction, made it a crime to prevent someone from voting because of his race. Other paramilitary groups, such as the <a href="/wiki/Red_Shirts_(Southern_United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Red Shirts (Southern United States)">Red Shirts</a> in the Carolinas, however, had intimidated freedmen and suppressed the vote. Hayes was determined to preserve the law protecting black voters, and vetoed the appropriation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972162–163Hoogenboom1995392–402Richardson2001161_144-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972162–163Hoogenboom1995392–402Richardson2001161-144">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Democrats did not have enough votes to override the veto, but they passed a new bill with the same rider. Hayes vetoed that bill too, and the process was repeated three more times.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972162–163Hoogenboom1995392–402Richardson2001161_144-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972162–163Hoogenboom1995392–402Richardson2001161-144">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> Finally, Congress passed a bill without the offensive rider, but refused to pass another bill to fund federal marshals, who were vital to the enforcement of the Enforcement Acts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972162–163Hoogenboom1995392–402Richardson2001161_144-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972162–163Hoogenboom1995392–402Richardson2001161-144">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> The election laws remained in effect, but the funds to enforce them were curtailed for the time being.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995402_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995402-145">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hayes tried to reconcile the social <a href="/wiki/Mores" title="Mores">mores</a> of the South with the recently passed civil rights laws by distributing patronage among southern Democrats. "My task was to wipe out the color line, to abolish sectionalism, to end the war and bring peace," he wrote in his diary. "To do this, I was ready to resort to unusual measures and to risk my own standing and reputation within my party and the country."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005418_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005418-146">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> All his efforts were in vain; Hayes failed to persuade the South to accept legal racial equality or to convince Congress to appropriate funds to enforce the <a href="/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1875" title="Civil Rights Act of 1875">civil rights laws</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995317–318_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995317–318-147">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Civil_service_reform">Civil service reform</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Civil service reform">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Hayes took office determined to reform the system of civil service appointments, which had been based on the <a href="/wiki/Spoils_system" title="Spoils system">spoils system</a> since <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a>'s presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETrefousse200293–94_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETrefousse200293–94-148">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup> Instead of giving federal jobs to political supporters, Hayes wished to award them by merit according to an <a href="/wiki/Civil_service_examination" class="mw-redirect" title="Civil service examination">examination</a> that all applicants would take.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995318–319_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995318–319-151">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Hayes's call for reform immediately brought him into conflict with the <a href="/wiki/Stalwart_(politics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Stalwart (politics)">Stalwart</a>, or pro-spoils, branch of the Republican party. Senators of both parties were accustomed to being consulted about political appointments and turned against Hayes. Foremost among his enemies was New York Senator <a href="/wiki/Roscoe_Conkling" title="Roscoe Conkling">Roscoe Conkling</a>, who fought Hayes's reform efforts at every turn.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972164–165_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972164–165-152">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>To show his commitment to reform, Hayes appointed one of the best-known advocates of reform at the time, <a href="/wiki/Carl_Schurz" title="Carl Schurz">Carl Schurz</a>, to be <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Interior" title="United States Secretary of the Interior">Secretary of the Interior</a> and asked Schurz and <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Secretary of State">Secretary of State</a> <a href="/wiki/William_M._Evarts" title="William M. Evarts">William M. Evarts</a> to lead a special cabinet committee charged with drawing up new rules for federal appointments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPaul199871_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPaul199871-153">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Treasury" title="United States Secretary of the Treasury">Treasury Secretary</a> <a href="/wiki/John_Sherman" title="John Sherman">John Sherman</a> ordered <a href="/wiki/John_Jay_(lawyer)" title="John Jay (lawyer)">John Jay</a> to investigate the <a href="/wiki/New_York_Custom_House" class="mw-redirect" title="New York Custom House">New York Custom House</a>, which was stacked with Conkling's spoilsmen.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995318–319_151-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995318–319-151">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Jay's report suggested that the New York Custom House was so overstaffed with political appointees that 20% of the employees were expendable.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995322–325Davison1972164–165Trefousse200295–96_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995322–325Davison1972164–165Trefousse200295–96-154">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Arthur_NYcustoms_house_cartoon.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Cartoon of one man kicking another out of a building" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Arthur_NYcustoms_house_cartoon.jpg/220px-Arthur_NYcustoms_house_cartoon.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="178" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Arthur_NYcustoms_house_cartoon.jpg/330px-Arthur_NYcustoms_house_cartoon.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Arthur_NYcustoms_house_cartoon.jpg/440px-Arthur_NYcustoms_house_cartoon.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1567" data-file-height="1269" /></a><figcaption>A cartoon of Hayes kicking <a href="/wiki/Chester_A._Arthur" title="Chester A. Arthur">Chester A. Arthur</a> out of the New York Custom House</figcaption></figure>
<p>Although he could not convince Congress to prohibit the spoils system, Hayes issued an <a href="/wiki/Executive_order_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Executive order (United States)">executive order</a> that forbade federal office holders from being required to make campaign contributions or otherwise taking part in party politics.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995322–325Davison1972164–165Trefousse200295–96_154-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995322–325Davison1972164–165Trefousse200295–96-154">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Chester_A._Arthur" title="Chester A. Arthur">Chester A. Arthur</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Collector_of_the_Port_of_New_York" title="Collector of the Port of New York">Collector of the Port of New York</a>, and his subordinates <a href="/wiki/Alonzo_B._Cornell" title="Alonzo B. Cornell">Alonzo B. Cornell</a> and <a href="/wiki/George_H._Sharpe" title="George H. Sharpe">George H. Sharpe</a>, all Conkling supporters, refused to obey the order.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995322–325Davison1972164–165Trefousse200295–96_154-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995322–325Davison1972164–165Trefousse200295–96-154">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> In September 1877, Hayes demanded their resignations, which they refused to give. He submitted appointments of <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt,_Sr." class="mw-redirect" title="Theodore Roosevelt, Sr.">Theodore Roosevelt, Sr.</a>, <a href="/wiki/L._Bradford_Prince" title="L. Bradford Prince">L. Bradford Prince</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Edwin_Atkins_Merritt" title="Edwin Atkins Merritt">Edwin Merritt</a>—all supporters of Evarts, Conkling's New York rival—to the Senate for confirmation as their replacements.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995352Trefousse200295–96_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995352Trefousse200295–96-155">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> The Senate's Commerce Committee, chaired by Conkling, voted unanimously to reject the nominees. The full Senate rejected Roosevelt and Prince by a vote of 31–25, and confirmed Merritt only because Sharpe's term had expired.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995353–355Trefousse2002100–101_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995353–355Trefousse2002100–101-156">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hayes was forced to wait until July 1878, when he fired Arthur and Cornell during a Congressional recess and replaced them with <a href="/wiki/Recess_appointment" title="Recess appointment">recess appointments</a> of Merritt and <a href="/wiki/Silas_W._Burt" title="Silas W. Burt">Silas W. Burt</a>, respectively.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995370–371_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995370–371-157">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;f&#93;</a></sup> Conkling opposed confirmation of the appointees when the Senate reconvened in February 1879, but Merritt was approved by a vote of 31–25 and Burt by 31–19, giving Hayes his most significant civil service reform victory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995382–384Barnard2005456_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995382–384Barnard2005456-160">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For the remainder of his term, Hayes pressed Congress to enact permanent reform legislation and fund the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Civil_Service_Commission" title="United States Civil Service Commission">United States Civil Service Commission</a>, even using his last <a href="/wiki/State_of_the_Union_address" class="mw-redirect" title="State of the Union address">annual message</a> to Congress in 1880 to appeal for reform. Reform legislation did not pass during Hayes's presidency, but his advocacy provided "a significant precedent as well as the political impetus for the <a href="/wiki/Pendleton_Civil_Service_Reform_Act" title="Pendleton Civil Service Reform Act">Pendleton Act</a> of 1883," which was signed into law by President Chester Arthur.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPaul199873–74_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPaul199873–74-161">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> Hayes allowed some exceptions to the ban on assessments, permitting <a href="/wiki/George_Congdon_Gorham" title="George Congdon Gorham">George Congdon Gorham</a>, secretary of the Republican Congressional Committee, to solicit campaign contributions from federal officeholders during the Congressional elections of 1878.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESproat1974165–166_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESproat1974165–166-162">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> In 1880, Hayes quickly forced <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Navy" title="United States Secretary of the Navy">Secretary of the Navy</a> <a href="/wiki/Richard_W._Thompson" title="Richard W. Thompson">Richard W. Thompson</a> to resign after Thompson accepted a $25,000 (~$615,229 in 2021) salary for a nominal job offered by French engineer <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_de_Lesseps" title="Ferdinand de Lesseps">Ferdinand de Lesseps</a> to promote a French canal in Panama.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESproat1974169–170_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESproat1974169–170-163">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hayes also dealt with <a href="/wiki/Star_route_scandal" class="mw-redirect" title="Star route scandal">corruption in the postal service</a>. In 1880, Schurz and Senator <a href="/wiki/John_A._Logan" title="John A. Logan">John A. Logan</a> asked Hayes to shut down the "<a href="/wiki/Star_routes" title="Star routes">star route</a>" rings, a system of corrupt contract profiteering in the Postal Service, and to fire Second Assistant Postmaster-General <a href="/wiki/Thomas_J._Brady" title="Thomas J. Brady">Thomas J. Brady</a>, the alleged ringleader.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlotsche1935409–411_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlotsche1935409–411-164">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> Hayes stopped granting new star route contracts but let existing contracts continue to be enforced.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995439–440_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995439–440-165">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> Democrats accused him of delaying proper investigation so as not to damage Republicans' chances in the 1880 elections but did not press the issue in their campaign literature, as members of both parties were implicated in the corruption.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlotsche1935409–411_164-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlotsche1935409–411-164">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> Historian <a href="/wiki/Hans_L._Trefousse" title="Hans L. Trefousse">Hans L. Trefousse</a> later wrote that Hayes "hardly knew the chief suspect [Brady] and certainly had no connection with the [star route] corruption."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETrefousse2002144_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETrefousse2002144-166">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> Although Hayes and the Congress both investigated the contracts and found no compelling evidence of wrongdoing, Brady and others were indicted for conspiracy in 1882.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlotsche1935414–415_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlotsche1935414–415-167">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> After two trials, the defendants were acquitted in 1883.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlotsche1935416_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlotsche1935416-168">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Great_Railroad_Strike">Great Railroad Strike</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Great Railroad Strike">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Great_Railroad_Strike_of_1877" title="Great Railroad Strike of 1877">Great Railroad Strike of 1877</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Harpers_8_11_1877_Destruction_of_the_Union_Depot.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A burning building" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Harpers_8_11_1877_Destruction_of_the_Union_Depot.jpg/220px-Harpers_8_11_1877_Destruction_of_the_Union_Depot.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="148" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Harpers_8_11_1877_Destruction_of_the_Union_Depot.jpg/330px-Harpers_8_11_1877_Destruction_of_the_Union_Depot.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Harpers_8_11_1877_Destruction_of_the_Union_Depot.jpg/440px-Harpers_8_11_1877_Destruction_of_the_Union_Depot.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2437" data-file-height="1641" /></a><figcaption>Burning of Union Depot, Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, July 21–22, 1877</figcaption></figure>
<p>In his first year in office, Hayes was faced with the United States' largest labor uprising to date, the <a href="/wiki/Great_Railroad_Strike_of_1877" title="Great Railroad Strike of 1877">Great Railroad Strike of 1877</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFoner2002583Stowell19991–2Richardson2001121_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoner2002583Stowell19991–2Richardson2001121-169">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> To make up for financial losses suffered since the panic of 1873, the major railroads had cut their employees' wages several times in 1877.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995326–327_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995326–327-170">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> In July of that year, workers at the <a href="/wiki/Baltimore_%26_Ohio_Railroad" class="mw-redirect" title="Baltimore &amp; Ohio Railroad">Baltimore &amp; Ohio Railroad</a> walked off the job in <a href="/wiki/Martinsburg,_West_Virginia" title="Martinsburg, West Virginia">Martinsburg, West Virginia</a>, to protest their reduction in pay.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBruce198975–77Stowell1999117_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBruce198975–77Stowell1999117-171">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Strike_action" title="Strike action">strike</a> quickly spread to workers of the <a href="/wiki/New_York_Central_Railroad" title="New York Central Railroad">New York Central</a>, <a href="/wiki/Erie_Railroad" title="Erie Railroad">Erie</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Pennsylvania_Railroad" title="Pennsylvania Railroad">Pennsylvania</a> railroads, with the strikers soon numbering in the thousands.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995328–333Davison1972145–153Barnard2005445–447_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995328–333Davison1972145–153Barnard2005445–447-172">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> Fearing a <a href="/wiki/Riot" title="Riot">riot</a>, Governor <a href="/wiki/Henry_M._Mathews" title="Henry M. Mathews">Henry M. Mathews</a> asked Hayes to send federal troops to Martinsburg, and Hayes did so, but when the troops arrived there was no riot, only a peaceful protest.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBruce198993–94_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBruce198993–94-173">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> In <a href="/wiki/Baltimore" title="Baltimore">Baltimore</a>, however, <a href="/wiki/Baltimore_railroad_strike_of_1877" title="Baltimore railroad strike of 1877">a riot did erupt</a> on July 20, and Hayes ordered the troops at <a href="/wiki/Fort_McHenry" title="Fort McHenry">Fort McHenry</a> to assist the governor in suppressing it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995328–333Davison1972145–153Barnard2005445–447_172-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995328–333Davison1972145–153Barnard2005445–447-172">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Pittsburgh" title="Pittsburgh">Pittsburgh</a> exploded into riots next, but Hayes was reluctant to send in troops without the governor's request.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995328–333Davison1972145–153Barnard2005445–447_172-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995328–333Davison1972145–153Barnard2005445–447-172">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> Other discontented citizens joined the railroad workers in rioting.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStowell1999116–127Hoogenboom1995328_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStowell1999116–127Hoogenboom1995328-174">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> After a few days, Hayes resolved to send in troops to protect federal property wherever it appeared to be threatened and gave Major General <a href="/wiki/Winfield_Scott_Hancock" title="Winfield Scott Hancock">Winfield Scott Hancock</a> overall command of the situation, marking the first use of federal troops to break a strike against a private company.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995328–333Davison1972145–153Barnard2005445–447_172-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995328–333Davison1972145–153Barnard2005445–447-172">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> The riots spread further, to <a href="/wiki/Chicago" title="Chicago">Chicago</a> and <a href="/wiki/St._Louis,_Missouri" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Louis, Missouri">St. Louis</a>, where strikers shut down railroad facilities.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995328–333Davison1972145–153Barnard2005445–447_172-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995328–333Davison1972145–153Barnard2005445–447-172">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By July 29, the riots had ended and federal troops returned to their barracks.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFoner2002585_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoner2002585-175">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> No federal troops had killed any of the strikers, or been killed themselves, but clashes between state militia troops and strikers resulted in deaths on both sides.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972148–150Trefousse200295_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972148–150Trefousse200295-176">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> The railroads were victorious in the short term, as the workers returned to their jobs and some wage cuts remained in effect. But the public blamed the railroads for the strikes and violence, and they were compelled to improve working conditions and make no further cuts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995334Davison1972152–153_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995334Davison1972152–153-177">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> Business leaders praised Hayes, but his own opinion was more equivocal; as he recorded in his diary: 
</p>
<blockquote><p>"The strikes have been put down by <i>force;</i> but now for the <i>real</i> remedy. Can't something [be] done by education of strikers, by judicious control of capitalists, by wise general policy to end or diminish the evil? The railroad strikers, as a rule, are good men, sober, intelligent, and industrious."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005446–447Hayes1922440v._3_178-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005446–447Hayes1922440v._3-178">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Currency_debate">Currency debate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Currency debate">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John-Sherman-2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Black-and-white photograph of a man, seated" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/John-Sherman-2.jpg/220px-John-Sherman-2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="265" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/John-Sherman-2.jpg/330px-John-Sherman-2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/John-Sherman-2.jpg/440px-John-Sherman-2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2986" data-file-height="3595" /></a><figcaption>Treasury Secretary <a href="/wiki/John_Sherman" title="John Sherman">John Sherman</a> worked with Hayes to return the country to the gold standard.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Hayes confronted two issues regarding the <a href="/wiki/Currency" title="Currency">currency</a>, the first of which was the <a href="/wiki/Free_Silver" class="mw-redirect" title="Free Silver">coinage of silver</a>, and its relation to <a href="/wiki/Gold_standard" title="Gold standard">gold</a>. In 1873, the <a href="/wiki/Coinage_Act_of_1873" title="Coinage Act of 1873">Coinage Act of 1873</a> stopped the coinage of silver for all coins worth a dollar or more, effectively tying the dollar to the value of gold. As a result, the <a href="/wiki/Deflation" title="Deflation">money supply contracted</a> and the effects of the Panic of 1873 grew worse, making it more expensive for debtors to pay debts they had contracted when currency was less valuable.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995356_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995356-179">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> Farmers and laborers, especially, clamored for the return of coinage in both metals, believing the increased money supply would restore wages and property values.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUnger2008358_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnger2008358-180">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> Democratic Representative <a href="/wiki/Richard_P._Bland" title="Richard P. Bland">Richard P. Bland</a> of <a href="/wiki/Missouri" title="Missouri">Missouri</a> proposed a bill to require the United States to coin as much silver as miners could sell the government, thus increasing the money supply and aiding debtors.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972176–177_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972176–177-181">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/William_B._Allison" title="William B. Allison">William B. Allison</a>, a Republican from <a href="/wiki/Iowa" title="Iowa">Iowa</a>, offered an amendment in the Senate limiting the coinage to two to four million dollars per month, and the resulting <a href="/wiki/Bland%E2%80%93Allison_Act" title="Bland–Allison Act">Bland–Allison Act</a> passed both houses of Congress in 1878.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972176–177_181-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972176–177-181">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> Hayes feared the Act would cause <a href="/wiki/Inflation" title="Inflation">inflation</a> that would be ruinous to business, effectively impairing contracts that were based on the gold dollar, as the silver dollar proposed in the bill would have an intrinsic value of 90 to 92 percent of the existing gold dollar.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995358–360_182-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995358–360-182">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> He also believed that inflating the currency was dishonest, saying, "[e]xpediency and justice both demand an honest currency."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995358–360_182-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995358–360-182">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> He vetoed the bill, but Congress overrode his veto, the only time it did so during his presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972176–177_181-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972176–177-181">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The second issue concerned <a href="/wiki/United_States_Notes" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Notes">United States Notes</a> (commonly called <a href="/wiki/Greenback_(money)" class="mw-redirect" title="Greenback (money)">greenbacks</a>), a form of <a href="/wiki/Fiat_money" title="Fiat money">fiat currency</a> first issued during the Civil War. The government accepted these notes as valid for payment of taxes and tariffs, but unlike ordinary dollars, they were not redeemable in gold.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972176–177_181-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972176–177-181">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Specie_Payment_Resumption_Act" title="Specie Payment Resumption Act">Specie Payment Resumption Act</a> of 1875 required the treasury to redeem any outstanding greenbacks in gold, thus retiring them from circulation and restoring a single, gold-backed currency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972176–177_181-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972176–177-181">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> Sherman agreed with Hayes's favorable opinion of the Act, and stockpiled gold in preparation for the exchange of greenbacks for gold.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995358–360_182-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995358–360-182">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> But once the public was confident that they could redeem greenbacks for specie (gold), few did so; when the Act took effect in 1879, only $130,000 of the outstanding $346,000,000 in greenbacks were actually redeemed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETrefousse2002107_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETrefousse2002107-183">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> Together with the Bland–Allison Act, the successful specie resumption effected a workable compromise between inflationists and <a href="/wiki/Hard_money_(policy)" title="Hard money (policy)">hard money</a> men and, as the world economy began to improve, agitation for more greenbacks and silver coinage quieted down for the rest of Hayes's presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972177–180_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972177–180-184">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Foreign policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_only_one_barred_out_cph.3b48680.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A Chinese man sitting outside a locked gate" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/The_only_one_barred_out_cph.3b48680.jpg/220px-The_only_one_barred_out_cph.3b48680.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="239" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/The_only_one_barred_out_cph.3b48680.jpg/330px-The_only_one_barred_out_cph.3b48680.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/The_only_one_barred_out_cph.3b48680.jpg/440px-The_only_one_barred_out_cph.3b48680.jpg 2x" data-file-width="976" data-file-height="1060" /></a><figcaption>A political cartoon from 1882, criticizing Chinese exclusion</figcaption></figure>
<p>Most of Hayes's foreign-policy concerns involved <a href="/wiki/Latin_America" title="Latin America">Latin America</a>. In 1878, following the <a href="/wiki/Paraguayan_War" title="Paraguayan War">Paraguayan War</a>, he arbitrated a territorial dispute between <a href="/wiki/Argentina" title="Argentina">Argentina</a> and <a href="/wiki/Paraguay" title="Paraguay">Paraguay</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995416_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995416-185">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> Hayes awarded the disputed land in the <a href="/wiki/Gran_Chaco" title="Gran Chaco">Gran Chaco</a> region to Paraguay, and the Paraguayans honored him by renaming a city (<a href="/wiki/Villa_Hayes" title="Villa Hayes">Villa Hayes</a>) and a <a href="/wiki/Department_(country_subdivision)" class="mw-redirect" title="Department (country subdivision)">department</a> (<a href="/wiki/Presidente_Hayes_Department" title="Presidente Hayes Department">Presidente Hayes</a>) in his honor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995416_185-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995416-185">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> Hayes became concerned over the plans of <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_de_Lesseps" title="Ferdinand de Lesseps">Ferdinand de Lesseps</a>, the builder of the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Canal" title="Suez Canal">Suez Canal</a>, to construct a canal across the <a href="/wiki/Isthmus_of_Panama" title="Isthmus of Panama">Isthmus of Panama</a>, then part of <a href="/wiki/Colombia" title="Colombia">Colombia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995417–418_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995417–418-186">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> Worried about a repetition of <a href="/wiki/Second_French_intervention_in_Mexico" title="Second French intervention in Mexico">French adventurism in Mexico</a>, Hayes interpreted the <a href="/wiki/Monroe_Doctrine" title="Monroe Doctrine">Monroe Doctrine</a> firmly.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995420–421Barnard2005442_187-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995420–421Barnard2005442-187">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> In a message to Congress, Hayes explained his opinion on the canal: "The policy of this country is a canal under American control&#160;... The United States cannot consent to the surrender of this control to any European power or any combination of European powers."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995420–421Barnard2005442_187-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995420–421Barnard2005442-187">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Mexico_%E2%80%93_United_States_border" class="mw-redirect" title="Mexico – United States border">Mexican border</a> also drew Hayes's attention. Throughout the 1870s, "lawless bands" often crossed the border on raids into Texas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995335Barnard2005443_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995335Barnard2005443-188">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> Three months after taking office, Hayes granted the Army the power to pursue <a href="/wiki/Bandit" class="mw-redirect" title="Bandit">bandits</a>, even if it required crossing into Mexican territory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995335Barnard2005443_188-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995335Barnard2005443-188">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> Mexican president <a href="/wiki/Porfirio_D%C3%ADaz" title="Porfirio Díaz">Porfirio Díaz</a> protested the order and sent troops to the border.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995335Barnard2005443_188-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995335Barnard2005443-188">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> The situation calmed as Díaz and Hayes agreed to jointly pursue bandits and Hayes agreed not to allow Mexican revolutionaries to raise armies in the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995337Barnard2005444_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995337Barnard2005444-189">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> The violence along the border decreased, and in 1880 Hayes revoked the order allowing pursuit into Mexico.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995338_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995338-190">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Outside the <a href="/wiki/Western_Hemisphere" title="Western Hemisphere">Western Hemisphere</a>, Hayes's biggest foreign-policy concern dealt with China. In 1868 the Senate had ratified the <a href="/wiki/Burlingame_Treaty" title="Burlingame Treaty">Burlingame Treaty</a> with China, allowing an unrestricted flow of Chinese immigrants into the United States. As the economy soured after the <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1873" title="Panic of 1873">Panic of 1873</a>, Chinese immigrants were blamed in the American West for depressing workmen's wages.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995387_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995387-191">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> During the Great Railroad Strike of 1877, anti-Chinese riots broke out in <a href="/wiki/San_Francisco" title="San Francisco">San Francisco</a>, and a <a href="/wiki/Third_party_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Third party (United States)">third party</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Workingman%27s_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="Workingman&#39;s Party">Workingman's Party</a>, formed with an emphasis on stopping Chinese immigration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995387_191-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995387-191">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> In response, Congress passed the <a href="/wiki/Chinese_Exclusion_Act" title="Chinese Exclusion Act">Chinese Exclusion Act</a> in 1879, abrogating the 1868 treaty.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995388–389Barnard2005447–449Rhodes1919180–196_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995388–389Barnard2005447–449Rhodes1919180–196-192">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> Hayes vetoed the bill, believing that the United States should not abrogate treaties without negotiation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995388–389Barnard2005447–449_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995388–389Barnard2005447–449-193">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> The veto drew praise from Northeastern New England Republicans, but Hayes was bitterly denounced in the <a href="/wiki/Western_United_States" title="Western United States">Western United States</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995388–389Barnard2005447–449_193-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995388–389Barnard2005447–449-193">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> In the subsequent furor, Democrats in the House of Representatives attempted to <a href="/wiki/List_of_efforts_to_impeach_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="List of efforts to impeach presidents of the United States">impeach</a> him, but narrowly failed when Republicans prevented a <a href="/wiki/Quorum" title="Quorum">quorum</a> by refusing to vote.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995390–391_194-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995390–391-194">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> After the veto, <a href="/wiki/United_States_Assistant_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Assistant Secretary of State">Assistant Secretary of State</a> <a href="/wiki/Frederick_W._Seward" title="Frederick W. Seward">Frederick W. Seward</a> suggested that the countries work together to reduce immigration, and he and <a href="/wiki/James_Burrill_Angell" title="James Burrill Angell">James Burrill Angell</a> negotiated with the Chinese to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995390–391_194-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995390–391-194">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> Congress passed a new law to that effect, the <a href="/wiki/Chinese_Exclusion_Act" title="Chinese Exclusion Act">Chinese Exclusion Act</a> of 1882, after Hayes had left office.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995390–391_194-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995390–391-194">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Indian_policy">Indian policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Indian policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Schurz_Departing_Interior_Department.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Schurz_Departing_Interior_Department.jpg/220px-Schurz_Departing_Interior_Department.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="256" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Schurz_Departing_Interior_Department.jpg/330px-Schurz_Departing_Interior_Department.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Schurz_Departing_Interior_Department.jpg/440px-Schurz_Departing_Interior_Department.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="1049" /></a><figcaption>An 1881 political cartoon about <a href="/wiki/Carl_Schurz" title="Carl Schurz">Carl Schurz</a>'s management of the Indian Bureau</figcaption></figure>
<p>Interior Secretary <a href="/wiki/Carl_Schurz" title="Carl Schurz">Carl Schurz</a> carried out Hayes's <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas" title="Indigenous peoples of the Americas">American Indian</a> policy, beginning with preventing the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Department_of_War" title="United States Department of War">War Department</a> from taking over the <a href="/wiki/Bureau_of_Indian_Affairs" title="Bureau of Indian Affairs">Bureau of Indian Affairs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972184–185_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972184–185-195">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> Hayes and Schurz carried out a policy that included assimilation into white culture, educational training, and dividing Indian land into individual household allotments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETrefousse2002109Davison1972186–187_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETrefousse2002109Davison1972186–187-196">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> Hayes believed his policies would lead to self-sufficiency and peace between Indians and whites.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995341–343,_449–450_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995341–343,_449–450-197">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Dawes_Act" title="Dawes Act">allotment system</a> under the Dawes Act, later signed by President <a href="/wiki/Grover_Cleveland" title="Grover Cleveland">Grover Cleveland</a> in 1887, was favored by liberal reformers at the time, including Schurz, but instead proved detrimental to American Indians. They lost much of their land through sales of what the government classified as "surplus lands", and more to unscrupulous white <a href="/wiki/Speculation" title="Speculation">speculators</a> who tried to get the Indians to sell their allotments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStuart1977452–454_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStuart1977452–454-198">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> Hayes and Schurz reformed the <a href="/wiki/Bureau_of_Indian_Affairs" title="Bureau of Indian Affairs">Bureau of Indian Affairs</a> to reduce fraud and gave Indians responsibility for policing their reservations, but they were generally understaffed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995343–344,_449_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995343–344,_449-199">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hayes dealt with several conflicts with Indian tribes. The <a href="/wiki/Nez_Perce" title="Nez Perce">Nez Perce</a>, led by <a href="/wiki/Chief_Joseph" title="Chief Joseph">Chief Joseph</a>, began <a href="/wiki/Nez_Perce_War" title="Nez Perce War">an uprising</a> in June 1877 when Major General <a href="/wiki/Oliver_O._Howard" class="mw-redirect" title="Oliver O. Howard">Oliver O. Howard</a> ordered them to move to a <a href="/wiki/Indian_reservation" title="Indian reservation">reservation</a>. Howard's men defeated the Nez Perce in battle, and the tribe began a 1,700-mile retreat to <a href="/wiki/Canada" title="Canada">Canada</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995338–340_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995338–340-200">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> In October, after a decisive battle at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Bear_Paw" title="Battle of Bear Paw">Bear Paw</a>, <a href="/wiki/Montana" title="Montana">Montana</a>, Chief Joseph surrendered and <a href="/wiki/William_Tecumseh_Sherman" title="William Tecumseh Sherman">William T. Sherman</a> ordered the tribe transported to Indian Territory in <a href="/wiki/Kansas" title="Kansas">Kansas</a>, where they were forced to remain until 1885.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995340–341_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995340–341-201">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> The Nez Perce war was not the last conflict in the West, as the <a href="/wiki/Bannock_people" title="Bannock people">Bannock</a> <a href="/wiki/Bannock_War" title="Bannock War">rose up</a> in spring 1878 in Idaho and raided nearby settlements before being defeated by Howard's army in July.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972184–185_195-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972184–185-195">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> War with the <a href="/wiki/Ute_people" title="Ute people">Ute tribe</a> broke out in Colorado in 1879 when some Ute killed Indian agent <a href="/wiki/Nathan_Meeker" title="Nathan Meeker">Nathan Meeker</a>, who had been attempting to convert them to Christianity. The subsequent <a href="/wiki/White_River_War" class="mw-redirect" title="White River War">White River War</a> ended when Schurz negotiated peace with the Ute and prevented white settlers from taking revenge for Meeker's death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETrefousse2002123_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETrefousse2002123-202">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hayes also became involved in resolving the removal of the <a href="/wiki/Ponca" title="Ponca">Ponca</a> tribe from <a href="/wiki/Nebraska" title="Nebraska">Nebraska</a> to <a href="/wiki/Indian_Territory" title="Indian Territory">Indian Territory</a> (present-day <a href="/wiki/Oklahoma" title="Oklahoma">Oklahoma</a>) because of a misunderstanding during the Grant administration. The tribe's problems came to Hayes's attention after its chief, <a href="/wiki/Standing_Bear" title="Standing Bear">Standing Bear</a>, filed a lawsuit to contest Schurz's demand that they stay in Indian Territory. Overruling Schurz, Hayes set up a commission in 1880 that ruled the Ponca were free to return to their home territory in Nebraska or stay on their reservation in Indian Territory. The Ponca were awarded compensation for their land rights, which had been previously granted to the <a href="/wiki/Sioux" title="Sioux">Sioux</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995450–454Sproat1974173_203-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995450–454Sproat1974173-203">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> In a message to Congress in February 1881, Hayes insisted he would "give to these injured people that measure of redress which is required alike by justice and by humanity."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETrefousse2002124_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETrefousse2002124-204">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Great_Western_Tour_of_1880">Great Western Tour of 1880</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Great Western Tour of 1880">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Rutherford_B._Hayes.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Rutherford_B._Hayes.png/220px-Rutherford_B._Hayes.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="263" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Rutherford_B._Hayes.png/330px-Rutherford_B._Hayes.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/25/Rutherford_B._Hayes.png 2x" data-file-width="426" data-file-height="510" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of Rutherford B. Hayes by <a href="/wiki/Eliphalet_Frazer_Andrews" title="Eliphalet Frazer Andrews">Eliphalet Frazer Andrews</a>, 1881</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1880, Hayes embarked on a 71-day tour of the <a href="/wiki/Western_United_States" title="Western United States">Western United States</a>, becoming the second sitting president to travel west of the <a href="/wiki/Rocky_Mountains" title="Rocky Mountains">Rocky Mountains</a>. (Hayes's immediate predecessor, Ulysses Grant, visited Utah in 1875.) Hayes's traveling party included his wife and <a href="/wiki/William_Tecumseh_Sherman" title="William Tecumseh Sherman">William T. Sherman</a>, who helped organize the trip. Hayes began his trip in September 1880, departing from Chicago on the <a href="/wiki/Transcontinental_railroad" title="Transcontinental railroad">transcontinental railroad</a>. He journeyed across the continent, ultimately arriving in California, stopping first in Wyoming and then Utah and Nevada, reaching Sacramento and San Francisco. By railroad and stagecoach, the party traveled north to Oregon, arriving in Portland, and from there to Vancouver, Washington. Going by steamship, they visited Seattle, and then returned to San Francisco. Hayes then toured several southwestern states before returning to Ohio in November, in time to cast a vote in the 1880 presidential election.<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Hayes.27s_White_House"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Hayes's_White_House">Hayes's White House</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Hayes&#039;s White House">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Hayes and his wife <a href="/wiki/Lucy_Webb_Hayes" title="Lucy Webb Hayes">Lucy</a> were known for their policy of keeping an alcohol-free White House, giving rise to her nickname "Lemonade Lucy."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom19953Davison1972xv_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom19953Davison1972xv-206">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> The first reception at the Hayes White House included wine,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison197282Barnard2005480_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison197282Barnard2005480-207">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> but Hayes was dismayed at drunken behavior at receptions hosted by ambassadors around Washington, leading him to follow his wife's <a href="/wiki/Temperance_movement" title="Temperance movement">temperance</a> leanings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995384_208-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995384-208">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> Alcohol was not served again in the Hayes White House. Critics charged Hayes with parsimony, but Hayes spent more money (which came out of his personal budget) after the ban, ordering that any savings from eliminating alcohol be used on more lavish entertainment.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995385–386Barnard2005480_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995385–386Barnard2005480-209">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> His temperance policy also paid political dividends, strengthening his support among <a href="/wiki/Protestantism" title="Protestantism">Protestant</a> ministers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995384_208-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995384-208">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> Although Secretary Evarts quipped that at the White House dinners, "water flowed like wine," the policy was a success in convincing <a href="/wiki/Prohibition_in_the_United_States" title="Prohibition in the United States">prohibitionists</a> to vote Republican.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995458_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995458-210">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Administration_and_Cabinet">Administration and Cabinet</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Administration and Cabinet">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hayes_cabinet_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Several men sitting around a table" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Hayes_cabinet_2.jpg/400px-Hayes_cabinet_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="400" height="297" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Hayes_cabinet_2.jpg/600px-Hayes_cabinet_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Hayes_cabinet_2.jpg/800px-Hayes_cabinet_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1536" data-file-height="1140" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Currier_%26_Ives" class="mw-redirect" title="Currier &amp; Ives">Currier &amp; Ives</a> lithograph of the Hayes cabinet in 1877</figcaption></figure>
