<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Judicial_appointments">Judicial appointments</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Judicial appointments">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/List_of_federal_judges_appointed_by_Rutherford_B._Hayes" title="List of federal judges appointed by Rutherford B. Hayes">List of federal judges appointed by Rutherford B. Hayes</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Thomas_Stanley_Matthews_-_Brady-Handy.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Black-and-white photograph of a bearded man" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Thomas_Stanley_Matthews_-_Brady-Handy.jpg/170px-Thomas_Stanley_Matthews_-_Brady-Handy.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="215" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Thomas_Stanley_Matthews_-_Brady-Handy.jpg/255px-Thomas_Stanley_Matthews_-_Brady-Handy.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Thomas_Stanley_Matthews_-_Brady-Handy.jpg/340px-Thomas_Stanley_Matthews_-_Brady-Handy.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2824" data-file-height="3568" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Stanley_Matthews_(judge)" title="Stanley Matthews (judge)">Stanley Matthews's</a> confirmation to the Supreme Court was more difficult than Hayes expected.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Hayes appointed two <a href="/wiki/Associate_Justice_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States" title="Associate Justice of the Supreme Court of the United States">Associate Justices</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States" title="Supreme Court of the United States">Supreme Court</a>. The first vacancy occurred when David Davis resigned to enter the Senate during the election controversy of 1876. On taking office, Hayes appointed <a href="/wiki/John_Marshall_Harlan" title="John Marshall Harlan">John Marshall Harlan</a> to the seat. A former candidate for governor of Kentucky, Harlan had been <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Bristow" title="Benjamin Bristow">Benjamin Bristow</a>'s campaign manager at the 1876 Republican convention, and Hayes had earlier considered him for <a href="/wiki/Attorney_General_of_the_United_States" class="mw-redirect" title="Attorney General of the United States">attorney general</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972130–132_211-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972130–132-211">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> Hayes submitted the nomination in October 1877, but it aroused some dissent in the Senate because of Harlan's limited experience in public office.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972130–132_211-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972130–132-211">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> Harlan was nonetheless confirmed and served on the court for 34 years, voting (usually in the minority) for aggressive enforcement of the civil rights laws.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972130–132_211-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972130–132-211">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> In 1880, a second seat became vacant upon the resignation of Justice <a href="/wiki/William_Strong_(Pennsylvania_judge)" title="William Strong (Pennsylvania judge)">William Strong</a>. Hayes nominated <a href="/wiki/William_Burnham_Woods" title="William Burnham Woods">William Burnham Woods</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Carpetbagger" title="Carpetbagger">carpetbagger</a> Republican <a href="/wiki/Circuit_court" title="Circuit court">circuit court</a> judge from <a href="/wiki/Alabama" title="Alabama">Alabama</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972132Hoogenboom1995454_212-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972132Hoogenboom1995454-212">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> Woods served six years on the Court, ultimately proving a disappointment to Hayes as he interpreted the Constitution in a manner more similar to that of Southern Democrats than to Hayes's own preferences.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005268,_498_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005268,_498-213">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hayes unsuccessfully attempted to fill a third vacancy in 1881. Justice <a href="/wiki/Noah_Haynes_Swayne" title="Noah Haynes Swayne">Noah Haynes Swayne</a> resigned with the expectation that Hayes would fill his seat by appointing <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Matthews_(judge)" title="Stanley Matthews (judge)">Stanley Matthews</a>, a friend of both men.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972129_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972129-214">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> Many senators objected to the appointment, believing that Matthews was too close to corporate and railroad interests, especially those of <a href="/wiki/Jay_Gould" title="Jay Gould">Jay Gould</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005498–499_215-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005498–499-215">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> and the Senate adjourned without voting on the nomination.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972129_214-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972129-214">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> The following year, when <a href="/wiki/James_A._Garfield" title="James A. Garfield">James A. Garfield</a> entered the White House, he resubmitted Matthews's nomination to the Senate, which this time confirmed Matthews by one vote, 24 to 23.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavison1972129_214-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison1972129-214">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> Matthews served for eight years until his death in 1889. His opinion in <i><a href="/wiki/Yick_Wo_v._Hopkins" title="Yick Wo v. Hopkins">Yick Wo v. Hopkins</a></i> in 1886 advanced his and Hayes's views on the protection of ethnic minorities' rights.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995457_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995457-216">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Post-presidency_.281881.E2.80.931893.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-presidency_(1881–1893)">Post-presidency (1881–1893)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Post-presidency (1881–1893)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Daniel_Huntington_-_Rutherford_Birchard_Hayes_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Painting of a bearded man, standing" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/76/Daniel_Huntington_-_Rutherford_Birchard_Hayes_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/170px-Daniel_Huntington_-_Rutherford_Birchard_Hayes_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="287" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/76/Daniel_Huntington_-_Rutherford_Birchard_Hayes_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/255px-Daniel_Huntington_-_Rutherford_Birchard_Hayes_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/76/Daniel_Huntington_-_Rutherford_Birchard_Hayes_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/340px-Daniel_Huntington_-_Rutherford_Birchard_Hayes_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3879" data-file-height="6544" /></a><figcaption>Official White House portrait of President Hayes by <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Huntington_(artist)" title="Daniel Huntington (artist)">Daniel Huntington</a>, 1884</figcaption></figure>
<p>Hayes declined to seek reelection in <a href="/wiki/1880_United_States_presidential_election" title="1880 United States presidential election">1880</a>, keeping his pledge not to run for a second term.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2017549_217-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2017549-217">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> He was gratified with the election of fellow Ohio Republican <a href="/wiki/James_A._Garfield" title="James A. Garfield">James A. Garfield</a> to succeed him, and consulted with him on appointments for the next administration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995447–465_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995447–465-218">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> After Garfield's inauguration, Hayes and his family returned to <a href="/wiki/Spiegel_Grove" title="Spiegel Grove">Spiegel Grove</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995466–467_219-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995466–467-219">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> In 1881, he was elected a companion of the <a href="/wiki/Military_Order_of_the_Loyal_Legion_of_the_United_States" title="Military Order of the Loyal Legion of the United States">Military Order of the Loyal Legion of the United States</a>. He served as commander-in-chief (national president) of the Loyal Legion from 1888 until his death in 1893. Although he remained a loyal Republican, Hayes was not too disappointed in Democrat <a href="/wiki/Grover_Cleveland" title="Grover Cleveland">Grover Cleveland</a>'s election to the presidency in 1884, approving of Cleveland's views on civil service reform.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995483_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995483-220">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> He was also pleased at the progress of the political career of <a href="/wiki/William_McKinley" title="William McKinley">William McKinley</a>, his army comrade and political protégé.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995524–525_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995524–525-221">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hayes became an advocate for educational charities and federal education subsidies for all children.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995471–475Thelen1970156_222-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995471–475Thelen1970156-222">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> He believed education was the best way to heal the rifts in American society and allow people to improve themselves.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThelen1970154–156_223-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThelen1970154–156-223">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> In 1887 Hayes was appointed to the Board of Trustees of <a href="/wiki/Ohio_State_University" title="Ohio State University">Ohio State University</a>, the school he helped found as governor of Ohio.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995498–499_224-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995498–499-224">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> He emphasized the need for <a href="/wiki/Vocational_school" title="Vocational school">vocational</a>, as well as academic, education: "I preach the gospel of work," he wrote, "I believe in skilled labor as a part of education."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005506_225-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005506-225">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> He urged Congress, unsuccessfully, to pass a bill written by Senator <a href="/wiki/Henry_W._Blair" title="Henry W. Blair">Henry W. Blair</a> that would have allowed federal aid for education for the first time.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESwint195248–49_226-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESwint195248–49-226">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> In 1889 Hayes gave a speech encouraging black students to apply for scholarships from the <a href="/wiki/Slater_Fund" title="Slater Fund">Slater Fund</a>, one of the charities with which he was affiliated.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995518–523_227-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995518–523-227">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> One such student, <a href="/wiki/W._E._B._Du_Bois" title="W. E. B. Du Bois">W. E. B. Du Bois</a>, received a scholarship in 1892.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995518–523_227-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995518–523-227">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> Hayes also advocated <a href="/wiki/Prison_reform" title="Prison reform">better prison conditions</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995496–497Thelen1970151_228-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995496–497Thelen1970151-228">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In retirement, Hayes was troubled by the disparity between the rich and the poor, saying in an 1886 speech, "free government cannot long endure if property is largely in a few hands and large masses of people are unable to earn homes, education, and a support in old age."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005513Hoogenboom1995539_229-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005513Hoogenboom1995539-229">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> The next year, he recorded thoughts on that subject in his diary:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote">
<p>In church it occurred to me that it is time for the public to hear that the giant evil and danger in this country, the danger which transcends all others, is the vast wealth owned or controlled by a few persons. Money is power. In Congress, in state legislatures, in city councils, in the courts, in the political conventions, in the press, in the pulpit, in the circles of the educated and the talented, its influence is growing greater and greater. Excessive wealth in the hands of the few means extreme poverty, ignorance, vice, and wretchedness as the lot of the many. It is not yet time to debate about the remedy. The previous question is as to the danger—the evil. Let the people be fully informed and convinced as to the evil. Let them earnestly seek the remedy and it will be found. Fully to know the evil is the first step towards reaching its eradication. <a href="/wiki/Henry_George" title="Henry George">Henry George</a> is strong when he portrays the rottenness of the present system. We are, to say the least, not yet ready for his remedy. We may reach and remove the difficulty by changes in the laws regulating corporations, descents of property, wills, trusts, taxation, and a host of other important interests, not omitting lands and other property.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHayes1922354v._4Swint195246–47_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHayes1922354v._4Swint195246–47-230">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
</blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_life_and_death">Later life and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Later life and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hayes_in_1886.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Hayes_in_1886.jpg/220px-Hayes_in_1886.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="295" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Hayes_in_1886.jpg/330px-Hayes_in_1886.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/f8/Hayes_in_1886.jpg 2x" data-file-width="336" data-file-height="450" /></a><figcaption>Hayes in 1886</figcaption></figure>
<p><span class="anchor" id="Death"></span>Hayes was greatly saddened by his wife's death in 1889.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995508–510_231-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995508–510-231">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> When she died, he wrote, "the soul had left [Spiegel Grove]".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995508–510_231-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995508–510-231">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> After Lucy's death, Hayes's daughter Fanny became his traveling companion, and he enjoyed visits from his grandchildren.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995509–520_232-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995509–520-232">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> In 1890, he chaired the Lake Mohonk Conference on the Negro Question, a gathering of reformers that met in upstate New York to discuss racial issues.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995515–517Foner2002605–606_233-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995515–517Foner2002605–606-233">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup> Hayes died of complications of a heart attack at his home on January 17, 1893, at the age of 70.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005522–523_234-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005522–523-234">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> His last words were "I know that I'm going where Lucy is."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005522–523_234-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnard2005522–523-234">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> President-elect Cleveland and Ohio governor McKinley, who would be Cleveland's immediate successor in 1897, led the funeral procession that followed his body until Hayes was interred in <a href="/wiki/Oakwood_Cemetery_(Fremont,_Ohio)" title="Oakwood Cemetery (Fremont, Ohio)">Oakwood Cemetery</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995532–533_235-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom1995532–533-235">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy_and_honors">Legacy and honors</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Legacy and honors">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hayes_grave_at_Spiegel_Grove.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Hayes_grave_at_Spiegel_Grove.jpg/220px-Hayes_grave_at_Spiegel_Grove.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Hayes_grave_at_Spiegel_Grove.jpg/330px-Hayes_grave_at_Spiegel_Grove.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Hayes_grave_at_Spiegel_Grove.jpg/440px-Hayes_grave_at_Spiegel_Grove.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1536" /></a><figcaption>Grave at Spiegel Grove</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the donation of his home to the state of Ohio for <a href="/wiki/Spiegel_Grove" title="Spiegel Grove">Spiegel Grove State Park</a>, Hayes was reinterred there in 1915.<sup id="cite_ref-NPS_2004-01-24_236-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NPS_2004-01-24-236">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup> The next year the <a href="/wiki/Rutherford_B._Hayes_Presidential_Center" title="Rutherford B. Hayes Presidential Center">Hayes Commemorative Library and Museum</a>, the country's first presidential library, opened on the site, funded by contributions from the state of Ohio and Hayes's family.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1980485–488_237-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1980485–488-237">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hayes had arbitrated and decided an 1878 dispute between Argentina and Paraguay in favor of Paraguay, giving Paraguay 60% of its current territory. This led to the naming of a province in the region after him: <a href="/wiki/Presidente_Hayes_Department" title="Presidente Hayes Department">Presidente Hayes Department</a> (capital: <a href="/wiki/Villa_Hayes" title="Villa Hayes">Villa Hayes</a>); an official holiday: <a href="/wiki/Laudo_Hayes_Firm_Day" title="Laudo Hayes Firm Day">Laudo Hayes Firm Day</a>, the anniversary of the decision, celebrated in Presidente Hayes province; a local soccer team: <a href="/wiki/Club_Presidente_Hayes" title="Club Presidente Hayes">Club Presidente Hayes</a> (also known as "Los Yanquis"), based in the national capital, <a href="/wiki/Asuncion" class="mw-redirect" title="Asuncion">Asuncion</a>; a postage stamp, the design of which was chosen in a contest run by the U.S. Embassy; and even the granting of the wish of a young girl who came out of a coma—a trip to the Hayes Presidential Center in Fremont, Ohio.<sup id="cite_ref-238" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-238">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Also named for Hayes is <a href="/wiki/Hayes_County,_Nebraska" title="Hayes County, Nebraska">Hayes County, Nebraska</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-239" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-239">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hayes was elected a member of the <a href="/wiki/American_Antiquarian_Society" title="American Antiquarian Society">American Antiquarian Society</a> in 1890.<sup id="cite_ref-240" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-240">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Rutherford_B._Hayes_High_School_(Delaware,_Ohio)" title="Rutherford B. Hayes High School (Delaware, Ohio)">Rutherford B. Hayes High School</a> in Hayes's hometown of Delaware, Ohio, was named in his honor, as is <a href="/wiki/University,_Hayes_and_Orton_Halls" title="University, Hayes and Orton Halls">Hayes Hall</a>, built in 1893, at the Ohio State University. It is Ohio State's oldest remaining building, and was placed on the National Register of Historic Places on July 16, 1970, due to its front facade, which remains virtually untouched from its original appearance. Hayes knew the building would be named in his honor, but did not live to see it completed.<sup id="cite_ref-241" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-241">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup> The City of Delaware also erected a statue of Hayes.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rutherford_B._Hayes&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
